Proof. Differentiate the BKAR functional along a tree. A hard-core derivative costs one overlap bond
and no field derivative. A covariance derivative costs one covariance connector and one field derivative at
each endpoint. If di covariance edges meet vertex i, the analytic row contributes di!σ−di
Aαi,N .
The Gram lemma bounds every auxiliary Gaussian regulator moment uniformly in the replica family.
Summing the choices of hard-core versus covariance derivatives costs only 2n−1
. The Pruefer lemma
shows that the exact 1/n! in the connected logarithm absorbs the product of vertex factorials with only
an exponential loss. The remaining rooted tree sum is the ordinary parent-normalized geometric recursion
in the effective link constant. Fixed structural marks are distributed by Leibniz and change only the
exponential constant depending on N.

4.3 Gaussian reference and optical covariance
This section constructs the Gaussian objects used by the weak block, Wick coordinates, replica forests,
and source connectors. Two objects must not be conflated:
1. the fixed tangent Feynman Gaussian on mean-zero lattice one-forms;
2. its exact conditional detail Gaussian for the linearized forty-nine-path Karcher block.
The first carries the massless retained field. The second is an optical, high-pass covariance with a uniform
gap. The nonlinear Wilson action, Haar and exponential-coordinate density, Karcher coarea factor, cutoffs,
inherited interaction, and source factors are not hidden in either covariance; they are exposed as exact
activities after the normal-fiber change of variables.

compatibility follow from covariance of the Karcher map, its normal section, the tree gauge, and the
scalar Feynman precision. Individual artificial Gaussian detail measures are not claimed to be reflection
positive.

Lemma 5.10 (Mixed one-loop topology and subtraction ledger). Fix one rooted support X and expand
the nonexceptional background two-point coefficient to first order in the invariant-graph tangent and to
the finite Wilson order required at one loop. A forest renormalization part means a proper connected

can carry an unsuppressed P⋆ log a term; every row has polynomial support multiplicity, and (Hfar) is
O(aN

Proof. A connected one-loop graph has one simple cycle; every other internal edge is a bridge. A connected
1PI subgraph cannot contain an internal bridge, so a nontrivial mixed renormalization part must contain
the full unique cycle. Contracting it removes the sole loop. This also settles nested and overlapping
forests: after one mixed contraction every outer quotient is loop-free, disjoint mixed parts are impossible,
and a forest with no mixed member leaves one marked loop. Lemma 5.9 supplies its degree-six kernel;
the unused support exponent absorbs the finite edge-pair and localization polynomials.

Lemma 8.6 (Exact multiscale Faà di Bruno forest formula). For every finite depth m and every fixed
derivative order n,
Dn
Wm(0)[J1, . . . , Jn] =
X
τ∈Tm,n
Am(τ; J1, . . . , Jn), (115)
where Tm,n is the finite family of recursively generated scale-labelled connected trees described above. Each
analytic coefficient of each exact conditional logarithm occurs exactly once. After absolute values are
taken, the sum is dominated uniformly in m by the rooted weak rows and the finite matching expansion.
Hence (115) passes to the infinite weak-depth limit termwise for every fixed n.
Proof. For one conditional integration, differentiation of the normalized logarithm is the joint-cumulant
identity. The set-partition formula for a derivative of a composition is precisely the Banach-valued Faà di
Bruno formula: every block of the partition is one incoming connected component and the outer cumulant
joins those blocks at the current scale. Fixing the least-label order makes this representation unique.
Induction over the finite sequence of exact conditional integrations proves (115); the terminal matching
logarithm is the final induction step. The weak rooted margin pays every nonroot weak vertex by σ < 1,
while the finite macrobridge and matching KP expansion pay the finitely many terminal vertices. The
one/two-root bounds control a component while its source leaves remain external. These majorants are
summable independently of m, so dominated convergence gives the last assertion.
For a tree carrying two distinguished source buffers, let v(A, B) be their unique lowest common ancestor.
If v(A, B) belongs to the terminal matching logarithm, call the history a matching history. Otherwise its
scale label j(v) is the unique weak coarsest-connection scale. Notice that this is a property of the exact
recursively generated tree, not a choice made after absolute values or bounds have been applied.
Corollary 8.7 (No omission and no double counting). The connected two-buffer part of D2
Wm(0) is
the disjoint sum of the matching histories and the weak histories indexed by their unique label j ≥ jw.
Contracting all strict descendants of v(A, B) produces a one/two-root decorated vertex whose norm is
bounded uniformly by Theorem 7.2 and Corollary 7.4. The decomposition remains exact after regular and
irregular sectors are recombined. Every connected tree has exactly one lowest common ancestor for the two
source leaves, and the recursive least-label convention gives it exactly one representation in Lemma 8.6.
Descendants have strictly finer labels or belong to the same finite conditional vertex and are summed by
its connected cumulant. Exact regular/irregular recombination precedes the conditional logarithm at every
scale, so the forest formula expands the full measure rather than a conditioned all-good measure.

carrying scale labels. Contract every maximal connected collection of finer-scale objects into a decorated
root and declare the largest physical scale remaining to be the coarsest scale of the history. Ties are
broken lexicographically.

Corollary 8.12 (Uniform source analyticity and exponential clustering). On a fixed neighborhood of the
origin, every finite source functional is analytic uniformly in resolution and volume. Thermodynamic
and zero-spacing limits of all fixed finite source derivatives exist along one common diagonal subsequence.
If two groups of source supports are separated by physical distance d, their connected full moments are
bounded by Ce−md
with the same m > 0 as in Theorem 8.9. The local source incidence and Lemma 8.3,
together with the finite compact matching expansion, give a common analytic source ball. Theorem 8.11
permits termwise differentiation and a countable diagonal extraction. The finite-volume approximants are
chosen on a cofinal schedule from Corollary 6.2. The extraction is made once for the countable algebra
and a fixed countable set of dyadic physical source scales, and the resulting subsequence is relabelled as
the directed sequence appearing in Theorem 1.1; no uniqueness or schedule-independence claim is needed.
In every connected partition joining two separated groups, at least one tree edge crosses the separation;
factor its exponential and sum all remaining edges with (118). Full moments follow from the set-partition
formula.

4.3 Gaussian reference and optical covariance
This section constructs the Gaussian objects used by the weak block, Wick coordinates, replica forests,
and source connectors. Two objects must not be conflated:
1. the fixed tangent Feynman Gaussian on mean-zero lattice one-forms;
2. its exact conditional detail Gaussian for the linearized forty-nine-path Karcher block.
The first carries the massless retained field. The second is an optical, high-pass covariance with a uniform
gap. The nonlinear Wilson action, Haar and exponential-coordinate density, Karcher coarea factor, cutoffs,
inherited interaction, and source factors are not hidden in either covariance; they are exposed as exact
activities after the normal-fiber change of variables.

Lemma 5.10 (Mixed one-loop topology and subtraction ledger). Fix one rooted support X and expand
the nonexceptional background two-point coefficient to first order in the invariant-graph tangent and to
the finite Wilson order required at one loop. A forest renormalization part means a proper connected

pure-Wilson packet; (H1) one marked insertion, with only pure-Wilson contractions or one mixed cycle
contraction; (H≥ 2) several marked insertions with the same alternatives; or (Hfar) a diam X > 1. A
44
mixed contraction is loop-free. A loop-bearing mixed quotient retains a marked kernel whose complete jet
through degree four vanishes and whose physical even component begins in degree six. Hence only (W)
can carry an unsuppressed P⋆ log a term; every row has polynomial support multiplicity, and (Hfar) is
O(aN
) for every fixed N after the rooted sum.
Proof. A connected one-loop graph has one simple cycle; every other internal edge is a bridge. A connected
1PI subgraph cannot contain an internal bridge, so a nontrivial mixed renormalization part must contain
the full unique cycle. Contracting it removes the sole loop. This also settles nested and overlapping
forests: after one mixed contraction every outer quotient is loop-free, disjoint mixed parts are impossible,
and a forest with no mixed member leaves one marked loop. Lemma 5.9 supplies its degree-six kernel;
the unused support exponent absorbs the finite edge-pair and localization polynomials.

Lemma 8.6 (Exact multiscale Faà di Bruno forest formula). For every finite depth m and every fixed
derivative order n,
Dn
Wm(0)[J1, . . . , Jn] =
X
τ∈Tm,n
Am(τ; J1, . . . , Jn), (115)
where Tm,n is the finite family of recursively generated scale-labelled connected trees described above. Each
analytic coefficient of each exact conditional logarithm occurs exactly once. After absolute values are
taken, the sum is dominated uniformly in m by the rooted weak rows and the finite matching expansion.
Hence (115) passes to the infinite weak-depth limit termwise for every fixed n.
Proof. For one conditional integration, differentiation of the normalized logarithm is the joint-cumulant
identity. The set-partition formula for a derivative of a composition is precisely the Banach-valued Faà di
Bruno formula: every block of the partition is one incoming connected component and the outer cumulant
joins those blocks at the current scale. Fixing the least-label order makes this representation unique.
Induction over the finite sequence of exact conditional integrations proves (115); the terminal matching
logarithm is the final induction step. The weak rooted margin pays every nonroot weak vertex by σ < 1,
while the finite macrobridge and matching KP expansion pay the finitely many terminal vertices. The
one/two-root bounds control a component while its source leaves remain external. These majorants are
summable independently of m, so dominated convergence gives the last assertion.
For a tree carrying two distinguished source buffers, let v(A, B) be their unique lowest common ancestor.
If v(A, B) belongs to the terminal matching logarithm, call the history a matching history. Otherwise its
scale label j(v) is the unique weak coarsest-connection scale. Notice that this is a property of the exact
recursively generated tree, not a choice made after absolute values or bounds have been applied.
Corollary 8.7 (No omission and no double counting). The connected two-buffer part of D2
Wm(0) is
the disjoint sum of the matching histories and the weak histories indexed by their unique label j ≥ jw.
Contracting all strict descendants of v(A, B) produces a one/two-root decorated vertex whose norm is
bounded uniformly by Theorem 7.2 and Corollary 7.4. The decomposition remains exact after regular and
irregular sectors are recombined. Every connected tree has exactly one lowest common ancestor for the two
source leaves, and the recursive least-label convention gives it exactly one representation in Lemma 8.6.
Descendants have strictly finer labels or belong to the same finite conditional vertex and are summed by
its connected cumulant. Exact regular/irregular recombination precedes the conditional logarithm at every
scale, so the forest formula expands the full measure rather than a conditioned all-good measure.
Unique-coarsest-scale allocation Differentiate the exact composition of normalized conditional
logarithms with respect to source parameters. Every connected term has a finite collection of local objects
carrying scale labels. Contract every maximal connected collection of finer-scale objects into a decorated
root and declare the largest physical scale remaining to be the coarsest scale of the history. Ties are
broken lexicographically.

local clusters and the same connector exponent applies. Thus the theorem derives a cutoff-independent
physical rate without suppressing or hiding contact terms.

Corollary 8.12 (Uniform source analyticity and exponential clustering). On a fixed neighborhood of the
origin, every finite source functional is analytic uniformly in resolution and volume. Thermodynamic
and zero-spacing limits of all fixed finite source derivatives exist along one common diagonal subsequence.

with the same m > 0 as in Theorem 8.9. The local source incidence and Lemma 8.3,

permits termwise differentiation and a countable diagonal extraction. The finite-volume approximants are
chosen on a cofinal schedule from Corollary 6.2. The extraction is made once for the countable algebra
and a fixed countable set of dyadic physical source scales, and the resulting subsequence is relabelled as
the directed sequence appearing in Theorem 1.1; no uniqueness or schedule-independence claim is needed.
In every connected partition joining two separated groups, at least one tree edge crosses the separation;
factor its exponential and sum all remaining edges with (118). Full moments follow from the set-partition
formula.

Lemma 8.14 (Reflection positivity inheritance under exact recombination). Every limiting positive-time
quadratic form used in the common Schwinger system is the limit of a reflection-positive quadratic form
under the original Wilson measure. Exact normal-chart, coarea, compact-pivot, and source transfor-
mations are changes of variables or observable identities, not replacement probability measures, and all
regular/irregular and half-cylinder labels are recombined before the OS form is taken. Therefore reflection
positivity passes to the common limiting family.
Proof. At finite cutoff and aligned reflection volume, let Fi be any finite family of constructed positive-time
observables. Pull each Fi back through the exact block, fiber, and source identities to a gauge-invariant

i,j
cicj⟨ΘFi Fj⟩a,L ≥ 0.
The equality is unchanged by the exact coordinate transformations because all Jacobian and normalization
factors remain inside the same normalized integral once. Every entry of the finite matrix converges along
the common subsequence, so the closed positive-semidefinite cone contains the limiting matrix. This
proves the claim.
Theorem 8.15 (Common positive-scale Schwinger system). The common subsequence defines tempered,
permutation-symmetric, reflection-positive, continuously translation-covariant Schwinger distributions for
the countable algebra generated by the bounded positive-scale observables and every fixed finite normalized
local source family. The distributions are projectively compatible and satisfy uniform physical exponential
clustering.
Proof. The Schwartz estimate gives equicontinuity on each finite tensor power. Diagonal extraction over
a countable dense test/source family gives the common projective system. Permutation symmetry passes
as an algebraic identity, while Lemma 8.14 gives the complete reflection-positivity mechanism after exact
label recombination. Riemann- sum translation errors vanish by the physical Schur bound, Corollary 8.12
gives clustering, and Lemma 8.13 gives the OS linear-growth row.
8.2 Ward identities and Euclidean covariance
Theorem 8.16 (Fixed-group invariant-jet admission through dimension six). For a compact simple Lie
algebra g:
(I1) the invariant symmetric bilinear forms form a one-dimensional space;
(I2) there is no reflection-compatible gauge-invariant local vector of engineering dimension at most four;
(I3) modulo total differences, equations of motion, and the reflection-odd topological density, the reflection-
even scalar dimension-four block is generated by the Yang–Mills density;
(I4) the gauge-invariant jet quotient through dimension six is finite. A primitive cubic symmetric color
tensor occurs precisely for Lie type Ar with r ≥ 2 and is retained in the dimension-six block; its
presence does not enlarge the dimension-four marginal block.
The filtered finite-matrix transport and associated-graded contraction used below therefore apply for every
fixed G after enlarging only the finite jet dimension.
Proof. Simplicity gives the uniqueness of the invariant bilinear form. Semisimplicity makes every finite-
dimensional representation traceless on g. Lorentz index counting, gauge covariance, and reflection parity
then exclude a single free vector index through dimension four and leave only the quadratic curvature
scalar in the even dimension-four sector. Chevalley’s theorem makes the invariant polynomial algebra
finitely generated; the degrees are the exponents plus one. Through degree three a primitive symmetric
tensor exists only for type Ar, and it first multiplies three curvatures, hence has engineering dimension
six. At every fixed engineering cutoff only finitely many covariant derivatives and invariant contractions
occur.

Lemma 8.10 (Labelled-tree extraction). Every connected source-labelled history admits a deterministic
allocation to a spanning tree on {1, . . . , n}. An edge joining bounded positive-scale roots is bounded by
Theorem 8.9(a). For renormalized local sources, every contact subtree is first contracted into one local
renormalized vertex; an edge joining two disjoint vertices is then bounded by Theorem 8.9(b). Only the
two endpoint clusters of one edge are retained as roots, and all previously exposed variables are exterior
data.
76
Proof. Start with the least label and expose the connected history until it first reaches the least unseen
label. Draw that edge, absorb the exposed component into the parent cluster, and repeat. Every new
label is attached once. When an edge is estimated, Theorem 6.44 treats the two endpoint clusters as the
only distinguished roots and is uniform in all already exposed exterior variables.

Proof. Differentiate the connected source logarithm and first collect each maximal contact subtree into

Corollary 8.12 (Uniform source analyticity and exponential clustering). On a fixed neighborhood of the
origin, every finite source functional is analytic uniformly in resolution and volume. Thermodynamic
and zero-spacing limits of all fixed finite source derivatives exist along one common diagonal subsequence.
If two groups of source supports are separated by physical distance d, their connected full moments are
bounded by Ce−md
with the same m > 0 as in Theorem 8.9. The local source incidence and Lemma 8.3,
together with the finite compact matching expansion, give a common analytic source ball. Theorem 8.11
permits termwise differentiation and a countable diagonal extraction. The finite-volume approximants are
chosen on a cofinal schedule from Corollary 6.2. The extraction is made once for the countable algebra
and a fixed countable set of dyadic physical source scales, and the resulting subsequence is relabelled as
the directed sequence appearing in Theorem 1.1; no uniqueness or schedule-independence claim is needed.
In every connected partition joining two separated groups, at least one tree edge crosses the separation;
factor its exponential and sum all remaining edges with (118). Full moments follow from the set-partition
formula.

Lemma 8.14 (Reflection positivity inheritance under exact recombination). Every limiting positive-time
quadratic form used in the common Schwinger system is the limit of a reflection-positive quadratic form
under the original Wilson measure. Exact normal-chart, coarea, compact-pivot, and source transfor-
mations are changes of variables or observable identities, not replacement probability measures, and all
regular/irregular and half-cylinder labels are recombined before the OS form is taken. Therefore reflection
positivity passes to the common limiting family.
Proof. At finite cutoff and aligned reflection volume, let Fi be any finite family of constructed positive-time
observables. Pull each Fi back through the exact block, fiber, and source identities to a gauge-invariant
function of the original positive-time links. Exact recombination makes this pullback independent of the
auxiliary labels. Reflection positivity of the Wilson measure gives
X
i,j
cicj⟨ΘFi Fj⟩a,L ≥ 0.
The equality is unchanged by the exact coordinate transformations because all Jacobian and normalization
factors remain inside the same normalized integral once. Every entry of the finite matrix converges along
the common subsequence, so the closed positive-semidefinite cone contains the limiting matrix. This
proves the claim.
Theorem 8.15 (Common positive-scale Schwinger system). The common subsequence defines tempered,
permutation-symmetric, reflection-positive, continuously translation-covariant Schwinger distributions for
the countable algebra generated by the bounded positive-scale observables and every fixed finite normalized
local source family. The distributions are projectively compatible and satisfy uniform physical exponential
clustering.
Proof. The Schwartz estimate gives equicontinuity on each finite tensor power. Diagonal extraction over
a countable dense test/source family gives the common projective system. Permutation symmetry passes
as an algebraic identity, while Lemma 8.14 gives the complete reflection-positivity mechanism after exact
label recombination. Riemann- sum translation errors vanish by the physical Schur bound, Corollary 8.12
gives clustering, and Lemma 8.13 gives the OS linear-growth row.

1 The compact-simple-group theorem and proof boundary
1.1 Problem and result
The constructive Yang–Mills problem asks for a nontrivial quantum gauge field theory on R4
satisfying
the Osterwalder–Schrader and Wightman requirements and possessing a strictly positive mass gap [1].
The physical scale, thermodynamic limit, zero lattice spacing, Euclidean covariance, local spectral content,
and non-abelian field geometry must be controlled in one compatible construction.
Fix throughout a compact connected simple Lie group G, a faithful unitary representation ρ : G → U(Vρ)
of dimension dρ, and a positive normalization of the invariant quadratic form for which the adjoint color
factor is denoted CA(G). The Wilson plaquette function is
wG,ρ(U) = cρ

1 −
1
dρ
Re Tr ρ(U)

,
where cρ > 0 is chosen once so that the Hessian at the identity is the quadratic form used by the
background-field coupling coordinate u = g2
. This choice is a finite linear normalization of the bare
Wilson coupling and is part of the fixed-group input register.
Theorem 1.1 (Four-dimensional Yang–Mills construction for fixed compact simple G). For every fixed
pair (G, ρ) as above, there is a directed sequence of reflection-positive Wilson lattice measures, with
microscopic spacing tending to zero and periodic volume tending to infinity, whose local gauge-invariant
curvature-jet Schwinger functions converge to a nontrivial Euclidean field theory on R4
.
The limiting Schwinger family is tempered, permutation symmetric, reflection positive, translation
continuous, exponentially clustering, and O(4) covariant. Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction yields a
positive-energy Poincaré-covariant local theory with a unique vacuum and a number m⋆(G) > 0 such that
H Ω⊥ ≥ m⋆(G).
On the centered invariant weak trajectory, the exact scale-two coupling map is
u(2L) = u(L) +
11 CA(G) log 2
24π2
u(L)2
+ OG(u(L)3
),
with uniform volume and cutoff control in the permanent weak regime. The reconstructed theory contains
a nonzero reflected local curvature vector and positive spectral mass in a finite interval [m⋆(G), M⋆(G)].
1.2 Fixed-group conventions and finite register
Write g = Lie(G) and dG = dim G. All Haar measures are normalized. The finite-volume approximants
use the ordinary periodic link space GE(Λ)
with periodic gauge transformations; no twisted boundary
condition or nontrivial finite-volume bundle sector is summed. This is sufficient for the claimed local
theory on the contractible space R4
. The bi-invariant metric, exponential coordinates, Laplacian ∆G,
Casimir eigenvalues, and operator norms are those determined by the declared quadratic form. In
curvature formulas, Tr(XY ) denotes this normalized invariant bilinear pairing; raw representation traces
are written Tr ρ(·). Let
sG :=

The fixed-group constant register CG consists of the finite quantities used below: the injectivity and
convexity radii, a positive local Karcher radius, finite holomorphic BCH and logarithm bounds through
order 2sG + 3, the faithful-action Hessian and complement charge, the Peter–Weyl constant, the finite
ordinary-periodic toron/seam template register, the adjoint color factor, and the finite invariant-jet
dimensions through the retained degree. Every statement called uniform is uniform in cutoff, volume,
directed depth, periodic wrapping, admitted exterior histories, and the finite reflected template family,
but its constants may depend on CG.
Proposition 1.2 (Reflection-positive Wilson input). For every finite periodic lattice Λ and u > 0, in the
ordinary periodic link sector,

Proof. The normalized character satisfies Re Tr ρ(U) ≤ dρ, with equality only when ρ(U) = I; faithfulness
gives U = e. The Hessian statement follows from the chosen normalization and compactness. Up to a field-
independent constant, each plaquette factor is exp{t(χρ +χρ)} with t > 0. Its power series is a nonnegative
linear combination of characters of tensor products of ρ and ρ̄, and their irreducible decompositions
have nonnegative integer multiplicities. The standard character-expansion reflection-positivity argument
therefore applies.
1.3 Proof architecture
The proof has four stages. First, the exact Karcher block is placed in a complete full-alias normal chart
and combined with a charged chart-complement partition, permanent analytic domain, marked connected
logarithm, Wick-stable jet block, and centered invariant graph. The folded spacetime symbol tensors
with Ig, while all nonlinear estimates are admitted by the finite register CG.
Second, exact background-scheme conjugacy identifies the universal marginal coefficient and a directed
schedule reaches the compact matching scale. The higher product Sobolev operator
Q
p(1−∆p)sG
replaces
the rank-one order and supplies the fixed-G Fourier–Wiener rows.
Third, complete Haar and optical variables are kept in one partition function. A mixed replica–
BKAR forest makes every root-to-root path pay separated Haar contraction, optical covariance decay,
or pre-existing support charge. Ordinary-periodic toron backgrounds, seams, reflections, and wrapping
certificates enter only through the finite template theorem of Appendix D.
Finally, a least-label source forest transports the complete invariant-jet system. The degree-four block
is group-independent because a compact simple Lie algebra has a unique invariant symmetric bilinear
form; possible cubic color tensors first enter the finite dimension-six block. Ward identities, rotation-defect
estimates, reflection-positive recombination, and Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction give covariance,
the unique vacuum, the gap, and noncollapse.

Only the theorem-level inputs in Table 1 are imported. The fixed-group geometry, action charge, higher
Sobolev landing, periodic-toron template theorem, invariant-jet admission, matching constants, and
noncollapse source are internal.

1.5 Self-contained relation to the rank-one construction
The companion SU(2) manuscript [14] is a completely expanded rank-one specialization and a useful
independent check. It is not a logical input to Theorem 1.1: every common analytic theorem used here is
stated and proved in this paper, and every new fixed-group admission is printed below. Appendix F gives
a theorem-level specialization map so that a referee can identify which arguments are color-free, which
tensor with Ig, and which require new group data.
1.6 Scope
The theorem concerns the local gauge-invariant curvature-jet observable sector for each fixed compact
connected simple G. It does not assert a bound uniform over groups, a simultaneous large-rank limit,
control of every extended Wilson-line or generalized-symmetry sector, or uniqueness of the continuum
limit outside the directed construction. Quotient groups are included through their actual compact link
group. The construction uses only the ordinary periodic finite-volume sector and retains all flat toron
backgrounds occurring in that sector; no claim about a sum over twisted boundary conditions is made or
needed.
2 Fixed-group Wilson geometry and Karcher blocking
Let Λ be a finite periodic four-dimensional lattice and use the reflection-positive Wilson measure of
Proposition 1.2. For each oriented retained link, use the straight two-link path with weight 7/8 and the
forty-eight signed-corner staples with weight 1/384 each. On a fixed strongly convex identity tube, define
the retained link as their weighted Karcher mean in the bi-invariant metric.
Lemma 2.1 (Fixed-group convex Karcher and holomorphic atlas). There are radii 0 < r0,G < r1,G
such that the squared-distance functions, product, inverse, exponential, logarithm, and weighted Karcher
critical point extend holomorphically to the corresponding local complexification tube. The Karcher Hessian
is bounded below there by a positive constant depending only on G. All derivatives through arbitrary
prescribed finite order are bounded on the smaller tube.
Proof. A compact Lie group with a bi-invariant analytic metric has positive injectivity and strong-convexity
radii. The sectional curvature is bounded because G is compact, and the Hessian comparison theorem
gives a positive lower Hessian bound on a sufficiently small ball. In a faithful matrix representation
the local complexification is an open neighborhood in the complexified group; multiplication, inversion,
exponential, and the principal logarithm are holomorphic there. The analytic implicit-function theorem
applied to the Karcher critical equation gives the holomorphic mean. Cauchy estimates on nested compact
tubes give every fixed derivative order; standard compact Lie-group analytic facts are summarized in
[15].
Proposition 2.2 (Exact block covariance and local submersion). The retained map is gauge covariant,
orientation covariant, signed-hypercubic covariant, and aligned-reflection covariant. Its linearization at
the flat connection has full retained rank. On one fixed weak tube it admits a finite-volume analytic
normal section whose matrix kernel and first three marked derivatives are exponentially local, uniformly
in volume.
Proof. Every path has the same endpoints, and the weighted squared-distance functional is invariant
under simultaneous left and right multiplication. Uniqueness of the Karcher minimizer gives gauge and
orientation covariance. The signed-corner path family is invariant under the lattice symmetry group.
At the flat point, the exact Fourier symbol is the color-free spacetime symbol tensored with Ig and has
acoustic value 2I ⊗ Ig and a uniformly gapped normal frame. The full-alias section and normal-fiber
contraction are proved in the later normal-chart chapters.
Proposition 2.3 (Exact coarea–fiber identity). At finite volume, in the normal fiber chart,
dU = J0 det(I + DN P)−1
dW dζ,

owned once as a s

1.5 Self-contained relation to the rank-one construction
The companion SU(2) manuscript [14] is a completely expanded rank-one specialization and a useful
independent check. It is not a logical input to Theorem 1.1: every common analytic theorem used here is
stated and proved in this paper, and every new fixed-group admission is printed below. Appendix F gives
a theorem-level specialization map so that a referee can identify which arguments are color-free, which
tensor with Ig, and which require new group data.
1.6 Scope
The theorem concerns the local gauge-invariant curvature-jet observable sector for each fixed compact
connected simple G. It does not assert a bound uniform over groups, a simultaneous large-rank limit,
control of every extended Wilson-line or generalized-symmetry sector, or uniqueness of the continuum
limit outside the directed construction. Quotient groups are included through their actual compact link
group. The construction uses only the ordinary periodic finite-volume sector and retains all flat toron
backgrounds occurring in that sector; no claim about a sum over twisted boundary conditions is made or
needed.
2 Fixed-group Wilson geometry and Karcher blocking
Let Λ be a finite periodic four-dimensional lattice and use the reflection-positive Wilson measure of
Proposition 1.2. For each oriented retained link, use the straight two-link path with weight 7/8 and the
forty-eight signed-corner staples with weight 1/384 each. On a fixed strongly convex identity tube, define
the retained link as their weighted Karcher mean in the bi-invariant metric.
Lemma 2.1 (Fixed-group convex Karcher and holomorphic atlas). There are radii 0 < r0,G < r1,G
such that the squared-distance functions, product, inverse, exponential, logarithm, and weighted Karcher
critical point extend holomorphically to the corresponding local complexification tube. The Karcher Hessian
is bounded below there by a positive constant depending only on G. All derivatives through arbitrary
prescribed finite order are bounded on the smaller tube.
Proof. A compact Lie group with a bi-invariant analytic metric has positive injectivity and strong-convexity
radii. The sectional curvature is bounded because G is compact, and the Hessian comparison theorem
gives a positive lower Hessian bound on a sufficiently small ball. In a faithful matrix representation
the local complexification is an open neighborhood in the complexified group; multiplication, inversion,
exponential, and the principal logarithm are holomorphic there. The analytic implicit-function theorem
applied to the Karcher critical equation gives the holomorphic mean. Cauchy estimates on nested compact
tubes give every fixed derivative order; standard compact Lie-group analytic facts are summarized in
[15].
Proposition 2.2 (Exact block covariance and local submersion). The retained map is gauge covariant,
orientation covariant, signed-hypercubic covariant, and aligned-reflection covariant. Its linearization at
the flat connection has full retained rank. On one fixed weak tube it admits a finite-volume analytic
normal section whose matrix kernel and first three marked derivatives are exponentially local, uniformly
in volume.
Proof. Every path has the same endpoints, and the weighted squared-distance functional is invariant
under simultaneous left and right multiplication. Uniqueness of the Karcher minimizer gives gauge and
orientation covariance. The signed-corner path family is invariant under the lattice symmetry group.
At the flat point, the exact Fourier symbol is the color-free spacetime symbol tensored with Ig and has
acoustic value 2I ⊗ Ig and a uniformly gapped normal frame. The full-alias section and normal-fiber
contraction are proved in the later normal-chart chapters.
Proposition 2.3 (Exact coarea–fiber identity). At finite volume, in the normal fiber chart,
dU = J0 det(I + DN P)−1
dW dζ,
5
where the determinant is the ordinary finite-dimensional determinant. The fixed linear determinant is
owned once as a scalar normalization, and the field-dependent logarithm is one connected analytic activity.

multiplication maps are entire, while inversion and the principal logarithm are holomorphic on a fixed
neighborhood of the identity. At the flat field the Karcher critical point has positive Hessian on the
retained tangent space; the analytic implicit-function theorem therefore supplies a unique holomorphic
Karcher mean on a fixed complex neighborhood. The path family and the reflected template family are
finite, so one may choose common nested radii 0 < η0 < η1 for all of them. On the larger finite-dimensional
complex polydisc, every elementary map and their finite compositions are holomorphic and uniformly
bounded. Cauchy’s integral estimate on the smaller polydisc gives uniform derivative bounds through
order three with denominator powers of η1 − η0.
The global block is obtained by translating this fixed finite cell. Each output row meets only a fixed
number of input cells, and each input column enters only a fixed number of output cells. Multiplying the
finite-cell Cauchy bounds by these fixed incidence numbers gives the two-sided exponentially weighted
Schur-kernel bounds. These incidence numbers do not depend on the total volume or preceding weak
depth. The aligned-refle

1.5 Self-contained relation to the rank-one construction
The companion SU(2) manuscript [14] is a completely expanded rank-one specialization and a useful
independent check. It is not a logical input to Theorem 1.1: every common analytic theorem used here is
stated and proved in this paper, and every new fixed-group admission is printed below. Appendix F gives
a theorem-level specialization map so that a referee can identify which arguments are color-free, which
tensor with Ig, and which require new group data.
1.6 Scope
The theorem concerns the local gauge-invariant curvature-jet observable sector for each fixed compact
connected simple G. It does not assert a bound uniform over groups, a simultaneous large-rank limit,
control of every extended Wilson-line or generalized-symmetry sector, or uniqueness of the continuum
limit outside the directed construction. Quotient groups are included through their actual compact link
group. The construction uses only the ordinary periodic finite-volume sector and retains all flat toron
backgrounds occurring in that sector; no claim about a sum over twisted boundary conditions is made or
needed.
2 Fixed-group Wilson geometry and Karcher blocking
Let Λ be a finite periodic four-dimensional lattice and use the reflection-positive Wilson measure of
Proposition 1.2. For each oriented retained link, use the straight two-link path with weight 7/8 and the
forty-eight signed-corner staples with weight 1/384 each. On a fixed strongly convex identity tube, define
the retained link as their weighted Karcher mean in the bi-invariant metric.
Lemma 2.1 (Fixed-group convex Karcher and holomorphic atlas). There are radii 0 < r0,G < r1,G
such that the squared-distance functions, product, inverse, exponential, logarithm, and weighted Karcher
critical point extend holomorphically to the corresponding local complexification tube. The Karcher Hessian
is bounded below there by a positive constant depending only on G. All derivatives through arbitrary
prescribed finite order are bounded on the smaller tube.
Proof. A compact Lie group with a bi-invariant analytic metric has positive injectivity and strong-convexity
radii. The sectional curvature is bounded because G is compact, and the Hessian comparison theorem
gives a positive lower Hessian bound on a sufficiently small ball. In a faithful matrix representation
the local complexification is an open neighborhood in the complexified group; multiplication, inversion,
exponential, and the principal logarithm are holomorphic there. The analytic implicit-function theorem
applied to the Karcher critical equation gives the holomorphic mean. Cauchy estimates on nested compact
tubes give every fixed derivative order; standard compact Lie-group analytic facts are summarized in
[15].
Proposition 2.2 (Exact block covariance and local submersion). The retained map is gauge covariant,
orientation covariant, signed-hypercubic covariant, and aligned-reflection covariant. Its linearization at
the flat connection has full retained rank. On one fixed weak tube it admits a finite-volume analytic
normal section whose matrix kernel and first three marked derivatives are exponentially local, uniformly
in volume.
Proof. Every path has the same endpoints, and the weighted squared-distance functional is invariant
under simultaneous left and right multiplication. Uniqueness of the Karcher minimizer gives gauge and
orientation covariance. The signed-corner path family is invariant under the lattice symmetry group.
At the flat point, the exact Fourier symbol is the color-free spacetime symbol tensored with Ig and has
acoustic value 2I ⊗ Ig and a uniformly gapped normal frame. The full-alias section and normal-fiber
contraction are proved in the later normal-chart chapters.
Proposition 2.3 (Exact coarea–fiber identity). At finite volume, in the normal fiber chart,
dU = J0 det(I + DN P)−1
dW dζ,
5
where the determinant is the ordinary finite-dimensional determinant. The fixed linear determinant is
owned once as a scalar normalization, and the field-dependent logarithm is one connected analytic activity.

retained tangent space; the analytic implicit-function theorem therefore supplies a unique holomorphic
Karcher mean on a fixed complex neighborhood. The path family and the reflected template family are
finite, so one may choose common nested radii 0 < η0 < η1 for all of them. On the larger finite-dimensional
complex polydisc, every elementary map and their finite compositions are holomorphic and uniformly
bounded. Cauchy’s integral estimate on the smaller polydisc gives uniform derivative bounds through
order three with denominator powers of η1 − η0.
The global block is obtained by translating this fixed finite cell. Each output row meets only a fixed
number of input cells, and each input column enters only a fixed number of output cells. Multiplying the
finite-cell Cauchy bounds by these fixed incidence numbers gives the two-sided exponentially weighted
Schur-kernel bounds. These incidence numbers do not depend on the total volume or preceding weak
depth. The aligned-reflection family is finite, so one takes a finite maximum.

Lemma 2.8 (Finite-cell analytic atlas and uniform Cauchy bounds). Let P49 be the finite path atlas
for one retained cell and its finite aligned-reflection template family. There exist fixed radii 0 < η0 < η1,
uniform under the standing convention, such that the product exponential, multiplication, inversion,
logarithm, and Karcher-mean maps defining every template extend holomorphically from Tη0
to Tη1
.
Write the canonically rescaled local block as
B(x) = Lx + N(x), N(0) = 0, DN(0) = 0.

retained tangent space; the analytic implicit-function theorem therefore supplies a unique holomorphic
Karcher mean on a fixed complex neighborhood. The path family and the reflected template family are
finite, so one may choose common nested radii 0 < η0 < η1 for all of them. On the larger finite-dimensional
complex polydisc, every elementary map and their finite compositions are holomorphic and uniformly
bounded. Cauchy’s integral estimate on the smaller polydisc gives uniform derivative bounds through
order three with denominator powers of η1 − η0.
The global block is obtained by translating this fixed finite cell. Each output row meets only a fixed
number of input cells, and each input column enters only a fixed number of output cells. Multiplying the
finite-cell Cauchy bounds by these fixed incidence numbers gives the two-sided exponentially weighted
Schur-kernel bounds. These incidence numbers do not depend on the total volume or preceding weak
depth. The aligned-reflection family is finite, so one takes a finite maximum.

Let TQ be a fixed smooth invariant dispersion function formed from the squared relative-loop distances of
the forty-nine path inputs. It vanishes on the flat set, its small sublevel set lies inside the common Karcher
chart, and it uses only one fixed enlarged-cell template. On that sublevel set the retained holonomies
Wp′ are defined by the local Karcher atlas. Multiply the displayed retained deficit by a fixed chart cutoff
that is one on a prescribed smaller Karcher sublevel and zero before the edge of the larger Karcher tube;
this gives a smooth invariant extension to the complete finite-cell configuration space. The threshold tch
below is chosen so that the entire curvature-label support lies where this cutoff equals one.
Lemma 3.1 (Representation-normalized charge and derivative ledger). For every fixed G, faithful ρ,
and integer 0 ≤ r ≤ 2sG + 3, there are finite constants depending only on the fixed cell atlas such that
wG,ρ(U) ≥ 0, wG,ρ(U) = 0 ⇐⇒ U = e,
cG∥X∥2
≤ wG,ρ(eX

(∥X∥ ≤ r0,G),

∥DwG,ρ(U)∥ ≤ CGwG,ρ(U)1/2
, ∥Dr
wG,ρ(U)∥ ≤ CG,r (2 ≤ r ≤ 2sG + 3).
The same bounds, with finite template constants, hold after composition with every Wilson word, retained
plaquette word, reflected word, or seam word used below.
Proof. Nonnegativity and the zero set are Proposition 1.2. In the chosen invariant metric the Hessian of
wG,ρ at the identity is positive definite; Taylor’s theorem gives the two-sided quadratic comparison. The
first-derivative estimate follows from that comparison on the identity tube. On its compact complement
wG,ρ has a positive minimum while its derivative is bounded, so the same inequality holds globally after
enlarging the constant. All higher derivatives are bounded because wG,ρ is a smooth matrix coefficient
on compact G. Every word in the fixed path atlas is a finite composition of multiplication and inversion,
so the chain rule gives the asserted finite derivative bounds.

enlarging the constant. All higher derivatives are bounded because wG,ρ is a smooth matrix coefficient
on compact G. Every word in the fixed path atlas is a finite composition of multiplication and inversion,
so the chain rule gives the asserted finite derivative bounds.

bulk and aligned-reflection templates proves the stated uniformity.

2.3 Nonlinear forty-nine-path section
Lemma 2.8 (Finite-cell analytic atlas and uniform Cauchy bounds). Let P49 be the finite path atlas
for one retained cell and its finite aligned-reflection template family. There exist fixed radii 0 < η0 < η1,
uniform under the standing convention, such that the product exponential, multiplication, inversion,
logarithm, and Karcher-mean maps defining every template extend holomorphically from Tη0
to Tη1
.
Write the canonically rescaled local block as
B(x) = Lx + N(x), N(0) = 0, DN(0) = 0.
For every exponential kernel rate 0 < m < mP and r = 2, 3, there is a finite constant
CN,r(m; η0, η1) < ∞
such that
sup
x∈Tη0
∥Dr
N(x)∥Km ≤ CN,r(m; η0, η1),
uniformly under the standing convention. The same constants, enlarged by a finite maximum, serve the
aligned-reflection templates.
Proof. For one retained cell the block depends on finitely many g coordinates. The exponential and
multiplication maps are entire, while inversion and the principal logarithm are holomorphic on a fixed
neighborhood of the identity. At the flat field the Karcher critical point has positive Hessian on the
retained tangent space; the analytic implicit-function theorem therefore supplies a unique holomorphic
Karcher mean on a fixed complex neighborhood. The path family and the reflected template family are
finite, so one may choose common nested radii 0 < η0 < η1 for all of them. On the larger finite-dimensional
complex polydisc, every elementary map and their finite compositions are holomorphic and uniformly
bounded. Cauchy’s integral estimate on the smaller polydisc gives uniform derivative bounds through
order three with denominator powers of η1 − η0.
The global block is obtained by translating this fixed finite cell. Each output row meets only a fixed
number of input cells, and each input column enters only a fixed number of output cells. Multiplying the
finite-cell Cauchy bounds by these fixed incidence numbers gives the two-sided exponentially weighted
Schur-kernel bounds. These incidence numbers do not depend on the total volume or preceding weak
depth. The aligned-reflection family is finite, so one takes a finite maximum.

wG,ρ(U)∥ ≤ CG,r (2 ≤ r ≤ 2sG + 3).
The same bounds, with finite template constants, hold after composition with every Wilson word, retained
plaquette word, reflected word, or seam word used below.
Proof. Nonnegativity and the zero set are Proposition 1.2. In the chosen invariant metric the Hessian of
wG,ρ at the identity is positive definite; Taylor’s theorem gives the two-sided quadratic comparison. The
first-derivative estimate follows from that comparison on the identity tube. On its compact complement
wG,ρ has a positive minimum while its derivative is bounded, so the same inequality holds globally after
enlarging the constant. All higher derivatives are bounded because wG,ρ is a smooth matrix coefficient
on compact G. Every word in the fixed path atlas is a finite composition of multiplication and inversion,
so the chain rule gives the asserted finite derivative bounds.

bulk and aligned-reflection templates proves the stated uniformity.

The irregular map must be assembled from the exact labelled density before any absolute-value estimate
is taken. This section fixes the ownership rule that was previously left as four composition hypotheses.
Let X = {X1, . . . , Xm} be a compatible family of bad cores. Compatibility means that the enlarged
integration cores I+
(Xi) are pairwise disjoint. Every pre-logarithmic local factor carries its actual finite
support; every exponentially local inverse, coarea, or Schur factor is first expanded in the permanent
exponential matrix algebra and each term is reported on its canonical connected hull.

If a fixed-template boundary factor meets an integration core only through its prescribed ring, it is
assigned to that core precisely when its canonical root lies in I(Xi); otherwise it remains regular. Ties
are therefore impossible.

Lemma 3.29 (History compatibility after exact output formation). At every RG step, form the complete
connected output first, assign its canonical reported hull, and only then compute its regulator allocation
and history certificate. Under this rule:
(i) every input history has one output owner;
(ii) no input allocation is copied to two incompatible output histories;
(iii) concatenation of successive certificates is associative;
(iv) summing over all certificates recovers the exact unlabelled connected output.
Proof. For one step, canonical collect is a partition of the finite set of labelled connected histories by the
least rooted hull in the fixed ordering. Thus there is one owner and the unlabelled sum is unchanged.
The output allocation is a function of the already collected output gradient and complex Hessian, not of
an input branch. Induct on the number of steps. At the next step the complete preceding certificate
is treated as one input label, so a new canonical hull is appended once. This proves associativity and
excludes copying. Forgetting the labels at every stage gives the exact iterated conditional logarithm by
Fubini and the connected exponential formula.

4.3 Gaussian reference and optical covariance
This section constructs the Gaussian objects used by the weak block, Wick coordinates, replica forests,
and source connectors. Two objects must not be conflated:
1. the fixed tangent Feynman Gaussian on mean-zero lattice one-forms;
2. its exact conditional detail Gaussian for the linearized forty-nine-path Karcher block.
The first carries the massless retained field. The second is an optical, high-pass covariance with a uniform
gap. The nonlinear Wilson action, Haar and exponential-coordinate density, Karcher coarea factor, cutoffs,
inherited interaction, and source factors are not hidden in either covariance; they are exposed as exact
activities after the normal-fiber change of variables.

Proof. Differentiate the BKAR functional along a tree. A hard-core derivative costs one overlap bond
and no field derivative. A covariance derivative costs one covariance connector and one field derivative at
each endpoint. If di covariance edges meet vertex i, the analytic row contributes di!σ−di
Aαi,N .
The Gram lemma bounds every auxiliary Gaussian regulator moment uniformly in the replica family.
Summing the choices of hard-core versus covariance derivatives costs only 2n−1
. The Pruefer lemma
shows that the exact 1/n! in the connected logarithm absorbs the product of vertex factorials with only
an exponential loss. The remaining rooted tree sum is the ordinary parent-normalized geometric recursion
in the effective link constant. Fixed structural marks are distributed by Leibniz and change only the
exponential constant depending on N.

The six one-step maps. The exact regular step is organized in the following order.
(E1) Normal chart return. Substitute the finite-volume analytic normal section into the local Wilson,
Haar, inherited, and source factors.

remainder.

one canonical hull, and collect exactly once.
(E6) Output recertification. Derive the next regulator allocation from the actual post-localization
output gradient and complex Hessian.

the ordered path product immediately gives (49). Haar invariance therefore annihilates the Haar part of
every nontrivial carrier at an uncovered pivot under the same integral in which the product reference was
obtained.

6.5.3 Final compact Haar-pivot collect
In (53), write U for the compact matching links and keep the complete optical field ζ and the actual
local source roots unchanged. Apply the exact compact-only path-product block BL to U as a finite
matching operation. Corollary 6.19 permits coefficientwise use of its hybrid chart, while every compact
path detail is integrated in the same complete joint partition function. No optical variable is identified
with a compact path detail, and no source is assumed fiber-constant under BL. The block is used once to
place many same-law Haar pivots inside each coarse compact edge.

Lemma 6.25 (Complete compact-flux classification and canonical exploration). Fix ζ and take the
Peter–Weyl decomposition of the complete compact incidence network generated by the exact pushforward
under BL. Integrate all compact variables coefficientwise. A connected history is compact-vacuum only
when every compact cut separating distinct mixed optical/source vertices carries the trivial representation.
After removal of already reported physical cross-core connectors, such a history factorizes into one-core
expectations or empty-support scalars and is assigned by the exact owner map to the normalized one-core
references or the pre-existing regular/Gaussian inventory.
Every remaining history, including a multi-leg singlet with trivial visible coarse label but nontrivial
internal flux, has a deterministic visited-set exploration. Order incident half-edges before marking an
unoriented edge inspected. Discovery edges form a rooted spanning tree; each merger, reconnection, shared
tail, cycle, seam, reflection, or wrapping edge is a uniquely owned closure edge. The record reconstructs
the occupied geometric network without duplicating shared continuations.
Proof. Vacuum factorization follows by deleting the reported physical connectors and applying Fubini
across every trivial compact cut. Otherwise the least mixed cut, least nontrivial representation label, and
least oriented half-edge choose the root and first edge. Deterministic depth-first exploration then classifies
every occupied unoriented edge exactly once as a discovery or closure edge. A composite singlet is retained
whenever any internal separating cut is nontrivial, independently of the visible output label.

toron/seam cover. On the covering lattice the local gauge trivialization sends every plaquette holonomy
to a conjugate of the corresponding fluctuation plaquette. The representation-normalized Wilson function
is conjugation invariant, so the charge and its differentiated finite-word ledger are unchanged by this
trivialization. The 2sG differentiated bad-core row of Theorem 3.20 is therefore uniform on the same
cover. Consequently the complete one-pivot score and Haar minorization retain their printed constants
after taking the finite template maximum. On intersections use the fixed least-label owner, so no factor is
duplicated. A wrapping certificate records only the four lattice directions, the fixed block range, a finite
seam crossing pattern, and an aligned reflection type; there are finitely many such local templates at one
normalized step. Their iteration is already controlled by the permanent wrapping ideal rather than by a
sum over topological sectors. Taking the finite maximum proves the stated admission and leaves the joint
Haar–optical boundary-to-center mechanism unchanged.
E Invariant tensor spaces through engineering dimension six
The low-dimensional color input can be stated without choosing a basis. The degree-two invariant
polynomial is unique for every simple g. A primitive degree-three invariant occurs only for Ar, r ≥ 2.
Hence the reflection-even dimension-four scalar block is one-dimensional for every G, while the dimension-
six block may contain the additional cubic curvature contraction in type A. The antisymmetric structure
111
tensor is always present through the bracket but contributes only inside the already retained covariant
derivative and commutator jets.
The manuscript uses the complete finite quotient generated by curvature, covariant derivatives, metric
and epsilon contractions, invariant color tensors, total-difference classes, and equation-of-motion contacts
through the declared engineering cutoff. No argument relies on a hand-picked SU(2) basis once this
quotient is used.
F Theorem-level specialization and dependency map
The purpose of this appendix is provenance rather than a new estimate. It identifies every independent
fixed-group input in the register CG, the first proof call at which that dependence enters, and the later
statements that consume it. In particular, the completion proof in Theorem 11.1 does not rely on an
unprinted constant or on a rank-one identity.

The table also separates the common mechanisms into four logical classes. Replica–BKAR, Kotecký–
Preiss, history compatibility, support reporting, least-label source forests, OS reconstruction, and spectral-
gap promotion are color-free. The full-alias symbol, normal right inverse, optical split, and spacetime
BPHZL degrees tensor with Ig. Karcher, action-charge, Peter–Weyl, and ordinary-periodic template
estimates enter only through the finite constants displayed above. Invariant jets and dimension-six color
tensors are enlarged finite spaces rather than rank-one identities.
The rank-one paper [14] is obtained by taking G = SU(2), sG = 1, and a trivial cubic invariant space.
This map is comparative; the present proof does not invoke the rank-one theorem.

exponential matrix algebra and each term is reported on its canonical connected hull.

templates. Taking the minimum of their radii gives ηcom > 0; Cauchy’s estimate on the closed half-radius
tube gives the uniform derivative constants.

Corollary 8.12 (Uniform source analyticity and exponential clustering). On a fixed neighborhood of the
origin, every finite source functional is analytic uniformly in resolution and volume. Thermodynamic
and zero-spacing limits of all fixed finite source derivatives exist along one common diagonal subsequence.

and a fixed countable set of dyadic physical source scales, and the resulting subsequence is relabelled as
the directed sequence appearing in Theorem 1.1; no uniqueness or schedule-independence claim is needed.

factor its exponential and sum all remaining edges with (118). Full moments follow from the set-partition
formula.

Lemma 8.14 (Reflection positivity inheritance under exact recombination). Every limiting positive-time
quadratic form used in the common Schwinger system is the limit of a reflection-positive quadratic form

proves the claim.
Theorem 8.15 (Common positive-scale Schwinger system). The common subsequence defines tempered,
permutation-symmetric, reflection-positive, continuously translation-covariant Schwinger distributions for
the countable algebra generated by the bounded positive-scale observables and every fixed finite normalized
local source family. The distributions are projectively compatible and satisfy uniform physical exponential
clustering.
Proof. The Schwartz estimate gives equicontinuity on each finite tensor power. Diagonal extraction over
a countable dense test/source family gives the common projective system. Permutation symmetry passes
as an algebraic identity, while Lemma 8.14 gives the complete reflection-positivity mechanism after exact
label recombination. Riemann- sum translation errors vanish by the physical Schur bound, Corollary 8.12
gives clustering, and Lemma 8.13 gives the OS linear-growth row.
8.2 Ward identities and Euclidean covariance
Theorem 8.16 (Fixed-group invariant-jet admission through dimension six). For a compact simple Lie
algebra g:
(I1) the invariant symmetric bilinear forms form a one-dimensional space;
(I2) there is no reflection-compatible gauge-invariant local vector of engineering dimension at most four;
(I3) modulo total differences, equations of motion, and the reflection-odd topological density, the reflection-
even scalar dimension-four block is generated by the Yang–Mills density;
(I4) the gauge-invariant jet quotient through dimension six is finite. A primitive cubic symmetric color
tensor occurs precisely for Lie type Ar with r ≥ 2 and is retained in the dimension-six block; its
presence does not enlarge the dimension-four marginal block.
The filtered finite-matrix transport and associated-graded contraction used below therefore apply for every
fixed G after enlarging only the finite jet dimension.
Proof. Simplicity gives the uniqueness of the invariant bilinear form. Semisimplicity makes every finite-
dimensional representation traceless on g. Lorentz index counting, gauge covariance, and reflection parity
then exclude a single free vector index through dimension four and leave only the quadratic curvature
scalar in the even dimension-four sector. Chevalley’s theorem makes the invariant polynomial algebra
finitely generated; the degrees are the exponents plus one. Through degree three a primitive symmetric
tensor exists only for type Ar, and it first multiplies three curvatures, hence has engineering dimension
six. At every fixed engineering cutoff only finitely many covariant derivatives and invariant contractions
occur.

(I3) modulo total differences, equations of motion, and the reflection-odd topological density, the reflection-
even scalar dimension-four block is generated by the Yang–Mills density;
(I4) the gauge-invariant jet quotient through dimension six is finite. A primitive cubic symmetric color
tensor occurs precisely for Lie type Ar with r ≥ 2 and is retained in the dimension-six block; its
presence does not enlarge the dimension-four marginal block.
The filtered finite-matrix transport and associated-graded contraction used below therefore apply for every
fixed G after enlarging only the finite jet dimension.
Proof. Simplicity gives the uniqueness of the invariant bilinear form. Semisimplicity makes every finite-
dimensional representation traceless on g. Lorentz index counting, gauge covariance, and reflection parity
then exclude a single free vector index through dimension four and leave only the quadratic curvature
scalar in the even dimension-four sector. Chevalley’s theorem makes the invariant polynomial algebra
finitely generated; the degrees are the exponents plus one. Through degree three a primitive symmetric
tensor exists only for type Ar, and it first multiplies three curvatures, hence has engineering dimension
six. At every fixed engineering cutoff only finitely many covariant derivatives and invariant contractions
occur.

Lemma 8.10 (Labelled-tree extraction). Every connected source-labelled history admits a deterministic
allocation to a spanning tree on {1, . . . , n}. An edge joining bounded positive-scale roots is bounded by
Theorem 8.9(a). For renormalized local sources, every contact subtree is first contracted into one local

Proof. Start with the least label and expose the connected history until it first reaches the least unseen

label is attached once. When an edge is estimated, Theorem 6.44 treats the two endpoint clusters as the
only distinguished roots and is uniform in all already exposed exterior variables.

Corollary 8.12 (Uniform source analyticity and exponential clustering). On a fixed neighborhood of the
origin, every finite source functional is analytic uniformly in resolution and volume. Thermodynamic
and zero-spacing limits of all fixed finite source derivatives exist along one common diagonal subsequence.
If two groups of source supports are separated by physical distance d, their connected full moments are
bounded by Ce−md
with the same m > 0 as in Theorem 8.9. The local source incidence and Lemma 8.3,
together with the finite compact matching expansion, give a common analytic source ball. Theorem 8.11
permits termwise differentiation and a countable diagonal extraction. The finite-volume approximants are
chosen on a cofinal schedule from Corollary 6.2. The extraction is made once for the countable algebra
and a fixed countable set of dyadic physical source scales, and the resulting subsequence is relabelled as
the directed sequence appearing in Theorem 1.1; no uniqueness or schedule-independence claim is needed.
In every connected partition joining two separated groups, at least one tree edge crosses the separation;
factor its exponential and sum all remaining edges with (118). Full moments follow from the set-partition
formula.

Lemma 8.14 (Reflection positivity inheritance under exact recombination). Every limiting positive-time
quadratic form used in the common Schwinger system is the limit of a reflection-positive quadratic form
under the original Wilson measure. Exact normal-chart, coarea, compact-pivot, and source transfor-
mations are changes of variables or observable identities, not replacement probability measures, and all
regular/irregular and half-cylinder labels are recombined before the OS form is taken. Therefore reflection
positivity passes to the common limiting family.
Proof. At finite cutoff and aligned reflection volume, let Fi be any finite family of constructed positive-time
observables. Pull each Fi back through the exact block, fiber, and source identities to a gauge-invariant
function of the original positive-time links. Exact recombination makes this pullback independent of the
auxiliary labels. Reflection positivity of the Wilson measure gives

cicj⟨ΘFi Fj⟩a,L ≥ 0.
The equality is unchanged by the exact coordinate transformations because all Jacobian and normalization
factors remain inside the same normalized integral once. Every entry of the finite matrix converges along
the common subsequence, so the closed positive-semidefinite cone contains the limiting matrix. This
proves the claim.
Theorem 8.15 (Common positive-scale Schwinger system). The common subsequence defines tempered,
permutation-symmetric, reflection-positive, continuously translation-covariant Schwinger distributions for
the countable algebra generated by the bounded positive-scale observables and every fixed finite normalized
local source family. The distributions are projectively compatible and satisfy uniform physical exponential
clustering.
Proof. The Schwartz estimate gives equicontinuity on each finite tensor power. Diagonal extraction over
a countable dense test/source family gives the common projective system. Permutation symmetry passes
as an algebraic identity, while Lemma 8.14 gives the complete reflection-positivity mechanism after exact
label recombination. Riemann- sum translation errors vanish by the physical Schur bound, Corollary 8.12
gives clustering, and Lemma 8.13 gives the OS linear-growth row.

Proposition 8.36 (Noncollapse and canonical normalization). The stress Schwinger distribution is
nonzero. Its normalization is fixed uniquely by the unit contact coefficient in (160); no independent finite
factor is available.
Proof. The reflected curvature source of Theorem 10.5 (proved independently in Section 10) defines

finite positive spectral measure supported away from zero; hence that correlation is not constant and its
distributional translation derivative is nonzero for suitable separated test profiles. Choose v supported
near one insertion. The right side of (160) is then nonzero, so the tensor insertion on the left cannot
vanish. Multiplying the tensor by a finite constant would multiply the left contact coefficient but not the
geometric derivation on the right, forcing the constant to be one.

algebra, with exact translation contacts and signed-hypercubic covariance. The later angular Ward theorem
promotes its continuum multiplet to O(4) covariance.
8.2.5 Euclidean rotation covariance
Low-dimensional rotation-defect classification.
Lemma 8.38 (No low-dimensional gauge-invariant vector). For every compact simple G there is no
nonzero local, reflection-compatible, gauge-invariant vector source of engineering dimension at most four,
modulo vacuum terms and total derivatives.
Proof. A connection mass term is not gauge invariant. At dimension two, Tr dρ(Fµν) = 0 by semisimplicity.
At dimension three, traces of dρ(DF) reduce to derivatives of Tr dρ(F) and vanish. A dimension-four
gauge-invariant vector cannot be formed from two curvature two-forms using only the metric while leaving
one free index; an epsilon tensor would have the wrong reflection parity. Total derivatives are excluded
by the stated quotient.
86
Lemma 8.39 (Antisymmetric stress defect begins at dimension six). A local signed-hypercubic, reflection-
even, gauge-invariant antisymmetric rank-two source has no component of engineering dimension four or
five. Its first possible nonzero continuum jet has dimension at least six.
Proof. At dimension four the quadratic curvature tensors are generated by Tr(FµαFνα), δµν Tr F2
, and
the corresponding dual-curvature contractions. The first is symmetric; the dual contraction is proportional
to a diagonal pseudoscalar tensor; none has an antisymmetric part. Reflection symmetry excludes the
odd-derivative dimension-five sector.
Corollary 8.40 (Vanishing rotation regulator). In the projective stress Ward identity, the antisymmetric
regulator insertion is a2
times a normalized family of dimension-six roots. The marked source bounds
therefore make it vanish in the zero-spacing limit. The cutoff rotation Ward identities integrate to full O(4)
covariance once the already established translation, reflection, and tensor-source continuity statements are
inserted.

Corollary 8.12 (Uniform source analyticity and exponential clustering). On a fixed neighborhood of the
origin, every finite source functional is analytic uniformly in resolution and volume. Thermodynamic
and zero-spacing limits of all fixed finite source derivatives exist along one common diagonal subsequence.
If two groups of source supports are separated by physical distance d, their connected full moments are
bounded by Ce−md
with the same m > 0 as in Theorem 8.9. The local source incidence and Lemma 8.3,
together with the finite compact matching expansion, give a common analytic source ball. Theorem 8.11
permits termwise differentiation and a countable diagonal extraction. The finite-volume approximants are
chosen on a cofinal schedule from Corollary 6.2. The extraction is made once for the countable algebra
and a fixed countable set of dyadic physical source scales, and the resulting subsequence is relabelled as
the directed sequence appearing in Theorem 1.1; no uniqueness or schedule-independence claim is needed.
In every connected partition joining two separated groups, at least one tree edge crosses the separation;
factor its exponential and sum all remaining edges with (118). Full moments follow from the set-partition
formula.

Theorem 8.15 (Common positive-scale Schwinger system). The common subsequence defines tempered,

8.2 Ward identities and Euclidean covariance
Theorem 8.16 (Fixed-group invariant-jet admission through dimension six). For a compact simple Lie
algebra g:
(I1) the invariant symmetric bilinear forms form a one-dimensional space;
(I2) there is no reflection-compatible gauge-invariant local vector of engineering dimension at most four;
(I3) modulo total differences, equations of motion, and the reflection-odd topological density, the reflection-
even scalar dimension-four block is generated by the Yang–Mills density;
(I4) the gauge-invariant jet quotient through dimension six is finite. A primitive cubic symmetric color
tensor occurs precisely for Lie type Ar with r ≥ 2 and is retained in the dimension-six block; its
presence does not enlarge the dimension-four marginal block.
The filtered finite-matrix transport and associated-graded contraction used below therefore apply for every
fixed G after enlarging only the finite jet dimension.
Proof. Simplicity gives the uniqueness of the invariant bilinear form. Semisimplicity makes every finite-
dimensional representation traceless on g. Lorentz index counting, gauge covariance, and reflection parity
then exclude a single free vector index through dimension four and leave only the quadratic curvature
scalar in the even dimension-four sector. Chevalley’s theorem makes the invariant polynomial algebra
finitely generated; the degrees are the exponents plus one. Through degree three a primitive symmetric
tensor exists only for type Ar, and it first multiplies three curvatures, hence has engineering dimension
six. At every fixed engineering cutoff only finitely many covariant derivatives and invariant contractions
occur.

6.5.1 Compact collect with optical field live
The matching theorem is now proved on one exact joint law, before the normal/optical variables are
marginalized. This is the decisive ordering. The compact retained variables are reduced conditionally by
Haar–pivot integration; the resulting activities remain functions of the complete optical field; and only
then is the connected logarithm taken by the marked Gaussian forest theorem. Thus no retained density,
latent mixture, or residual-only gas is inserted between the physical law and the connected source kernel.

Exact finite-depth multiscale forest formula For a microscopic depth m, let Rj denote the exact
normalized conditional-logarithm map that integrates the scale-j fluctuation field and returns the source-
decorated effective interaction at scale j − 1. The maps include the regular/irregular partition of unity
before the logarithm is taken, so no all-good denominator occurs. Write
Wm(J) = log Zm(J) − log Zm(0)
for the normalized finite-volume source logarithm. Iterating the exact maps Rm, . . . , Rjw
and then the
finite macrobridge is an identity, not an asymptotic expansion.
A scale-labelled source forest is generated recursively as follows. A source derivative is a labelled leaf.
At one conditional integration, connected cumulant differentiation joins a nonempty partition of the
incoming leaves and decorated subtrees to one new vertex labelled by that integration scale. Terms
transported without joining retain their existing components. At the terminal matching logarithm, the
remaining components are joined by the matching-scale connected cumulants. The ordering of children is
fixed by the least source label, so isomorphic terms are not counted more than once.
Lemma 8.6 (Exact multiscale Faà di Bruno forest formula). For every finite depth m and every fixed
derivative order n,
Dn
Wm(0)[J1, . . . , Jn] =
X
τ∈Tm,n
Am(τ; J1, . . . , Jn), (115)
where Tm,n is the finite family of recursively generated scale-labelled connected trees described above. Each
analytic coefficient of each exact conditional logarithm occurs exactly once. After absolute values are
taken, the sum is dominated uniformly in m by the rooted weak rows and the finite matching expansion.
Hence (115) passes to the infinite weak-depth limit termwise for every fixed n.
Proof. For one conditional integration, differentiation of the normalized logarithm is the joint-cumulant
identity. The set-partition formula for a derivative of a composition is precisely the Banach-valued Faà di
Bruno formula: every block of the partition is one incoming connected component and the outer cumulant
joins those blocks at the current scale. Fixing the least-label order makes this representation unique.
Induction over the finite sequence of exact conditional integrations proves (115); the terminal matching
logarithm is the final induction step. The weak rooted margin pays every nonroot weak vertex by σ < 1,
while the finite macrobridge and matching KP expansion pay the finitely many terminal vertices. The
one/two-root bounds control a component while its source leaves remain external. These majorants are
summable independently of m, so dominated convergence gives the last assertion.
For a tree carrying two distinguished source buffers, let v(A, B) be their unique lowest common ancestor.
If v(A, B) belongs to the terminal matching logarithm, call the history a matching history. Otherwise its
scale label j(v) is the unique weak coarsest-connection scale. Notice that this is a property of the exact
recursively generated tree, not a choice made after absolute values or bounds have been applied.
Corollary 8.7 (No omission and no double counting). The connected two-buffer part of D2
Wm(0) is
the disjoint sum of the matching histories and the weak histories indexed by their unique label j ≥ jw.
Contracting all strict descendants of v(A, B) produces a one/two-root decorated vertex whose norm is
bounded uniformly by Theorem 7.2 and Corollary 7.4. The decomposition remains exact after regular and
irregular sectors are recombined. Every connected tree has exactly one lowest common ancestor for the two
source leaves, and the recursive least-label convention gives it exactly one representation in Lemma 8.6.
Descendants have strictly finer labels or belong to the same finite conditional vertex and are summed by
its connected cumulant. Exact regular/irregular recombination precedes the conditional logarithm at every
scale, so the forest formula expands the full measure rather than a conditioned all-good measure.
Unique-coarsest-scale allocation Differentiate the exact composition of normalized conditional
logarithms with respect to source parameters. Every connected term has a finite collection of local objects
carrying scale labels. Contract every maximal connected collection of finer-scale objects into a decorated
root and declare the largest physical scale remaining to be the coarsest scale of the history. Ties are
broken lexicographically.

Corollary 8.12 (Uniform source analyticity and exponential clustering). On a fixed neighborhood of the
origin, every finite source functional is analytic uniformly in resolution and volume. Thermodynamic
and zero-spacing limits of all fixed finite source derivatives exist along one common diagonal subsequence.

and a fixed countable set of dyadic physical source scales, and the resulting subsequence is relabelled as
the directed sequence appearing in Theorem 1.1; no uniqueness or schedule-independence claim is needed.

factor its exponential and sum all remaining edges with (118). Full moments follow from the set-partition
formula.
